# Compendium of Essentials: Astrology
<note>The title <term>Sārasaṅgraha</term> literally translates to "a collection of the essence," which is a common genre of Indian literature designed to provide a concise summary of a larger field of study. <term>Jyotiṣa</term> is the traditional Indian science of "light" or celestial observation, encompassing both mathematical astronomy and predictive astrology.</note>

## 15 Folios (Fifteen)
<note>original: "patrāṇi 15 (pañcadaśāni)"; a <term>patra</term> refers to a single leaf of the manuscript. Writing the number in both digits and words was a standard archival practice to prevent any accidental or intentional alteration of the folio count.</note>

## ( Complete )
<note>original: "sampūrṇam"; this term confirms that the manuscript contains the entire text of the work from start to finish, indicating it is not a fragment or an isolated chapter.</note>

This label serves as a vital external reference for the manuscript. In traditional Indian libraries, manuscripts are often wrapped in red cloth (original: <term>bastā</term>) to protect the fragile paper or palm leaves from dust and light. This label allowed a librarian or scholar to identify the content, catalog number, and length of the work without needing to unwrap and handle the delicate original pages. This specific work, a compendium on **Astrology**, would have been used as a practical reference for calculating planetary positions or determining auspicious times for rituals.

# A Compendium of the Essence: Astrology
<note>The title <term>Sārasaṅgraha</term> (original: सारसंग्रहः) literally means "a collection of the essence," denoting a condensed handbook or digest. <term>Jyotiṣa</term> (original: ज्योतिषम्) refers to the traditional Indian system of astronomy and astrology.</note>

->Folios: 15 (Fifteen)<-

This label is a physical artifact of traditional Indian librarianship. To protect delicate paper or palm-leaf manuscripts from dust, light, and humidity, they were often wrapped in a **basta**—a square piece of protective textile, typically dyed red. 

Because the manuscripts were bundled and tied between wooden boards, it was impossible to see the title without unwrapping the entire package. Consequently, librarians pasted small paper slips like this one onto the outer cloth. This specific label identifies a manual on **Jyotiṣa**, an essential field of ancient Indian science used for calculating planetary positions and determining the most auspicious times for religious ceremonies and daily activities.

# The Compendium of the Essence: Astrology
<note>The title <term>Sārasaṅgraha</term> (original: सारसंग्रहः) translates to "a collection of the essence" or a "digest." It signifies a handbook that summarizes the most important points of a larger subject. <term>Jyotiṣa</term> (original: ज्योतिषम्) is the traditional Indian science of light, encompassing both astronomy and astrology.</note>

->Folios: 15 (Fifteen)<-
<note>original: <term>patrāṇi</term> (पत्राणि) literally means "leaves." In traditional Indian manuscripts, each leaf is a long, rectangular piece of paper or palm leaf. The number is provided in both numerals and the Sanskrit word for fifteen (<term>pañcadaśāni</term>) to ensure the inventory is accurate.</note>

->(Complete)<-
<note>original: <term>sampūrṇam</term> (संपूर्णम्); this confirms that all parts of the work are present within this bundle and it is not a fragment.</note>

This label is a physical artifact of traditional Indian librarianship, likely once pasted onto a red cloth wrapper known as a **basta**. These wraps were essential for protecting delicate manuscripts from dust, moisture, and insects. 

The text identified here, the **Sārasaṅgraha**, is a practical manual. In the ancient and medieval Indian world, **Jyotiṣa** (astrology/astronomy) was considered an "eye" of the Vedas—a crucial tool for calculating the correct times for rituals, agricultural cycles, and personal milestones. A "Compendium of the Essence" served as a convenient reference for a practitioner who needed to access complex astronomical calculations or omens without consulting a massive library of larger treatises.
